by parallel transport from
b_{*}\in U
. Then
\omega_{A},\omega_{B}:U\to\mathbb{C}
defined by (
418
) are holomorphic functions.

Proof.

Fix
U
simply connected. By Lemma
E.9
, after shrinking
U
we may choose a
C^{\infty}
trivialization
\Phi_{U}:\varpi^{-1}(U)\to U\times E_{b_{*}}
preserving the projection to
U
. Under
\Phi_{U}
, the transported cycles
(A_{b},B_{b})
correspond to fixed cycles
(A,B)\subset E_{b_{*}}
independent of
b\in U
. Choose a local holomorphic coordinate
w
on the fiber in a neighborhood of
A
and write, in that neighborhood,

\Omega=F(b,w)\,dw

(420)

with
F
holomorphic in
(b,w)
because
\Omega
is a relative holomorphic one-form. Parameterize
A
by a smooth map
\gamma_{A}:[0,1]\to E_{b_{*}}
and write
\gamma_{A}(t)=(w(t))
in the coordinate patch. Then for each
b\in U
,

\omega_{A}(b)=\int_{A_{b}}\Omega_{b}=\int_{0}^{1}F(b,w(t))\,w^{\prime}(t)\,dt.

(421)

Fix
b_{1}\in U
and let
\{b_{k}\}\subset U
be a sequence converging to
b_{1}
. Since
F
is holomorphic hence continuous and
[0,1]
is compact,
F(b_{k},w(t))\to F(b_{1},w(t))
uniformly in
t
. Therefore the integrands in (
421
) converge uniformly, and we obtain

\lim_{k\to\infty}\omega_{A}(b_{k})=\int_{0}^{1}\lim_{k\to\infty}F(b_{k},w(t))\,w^{\prime}(t)\,dt=\int_{0}^{1}F(b_{1},w(t))\,w^{\prime}(t)\,dt=\omega_{A}(b_{1}),

(422)

so
\omega_{A}
is continuous, the same argument applies to
\omega_{B}
. To prove holomorphicity, fix a small triangle
\Delta\subset U
with piecewise smooth boundary
\partial\Delta
, and compute

\int_{\partial\Delta}\omega_{A}(b)\,db=\int_{\partial\Delta}\left(\int_{0}^{1}F(b,w(t))\,w^{\prime}(t)\,dt\right)db.

(423)

Because
F
is continuous on the compact set
\overline{\Delta}\times\gamma_{A}([0,1])
, Fubini’s theorem for continuous integrands yields

\int_{\partial\Delta}\omega_{A}(b)\,db=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\int_{\partial\Delta}F(b,w(t))\,db\right)w^{\prime}(t)\,dt.

(424)

For each fixed
t
, the function
b\mapsto F(b,w(t))
is holomorphic on
\Delta
, hence
\int_{\partial\Delta}F(b,w(t))\,db=0
by Cauchy’s theorem. Substituting into (
424
) gives
\int_{\partial\Delta}\omega_{A}(b)\,db=0
. By Morera’s theorem,
\omega_{A}
is holomorphic on
U
. The same argument applies to
\omega_{B}
.
∎

Proposition E.12

(Conditional positivity
\Im\tau>0
)
.

Assume
(CA)
and use the setup of Proposition
E.11
on a simply connected
U
. Then
\omega_{B}(b)\neq 0
for all
b\in U
and
\tau(b)\in\mathbb{H}
for all
b\in U
.

Proof.

Fix
b\in U
. Since
\Omega_{b}
is a nowhere-vanishing holomorphic one-form on the elliptic curve
E_{b}
, the
(1,1)
-form
i\,\Omega_{b}\wedge\overline{\Omega_{b}}
is a positive volume form and

\int_{E_{b}}i\,\Omega_{b}\wedge\overline{\Omega_{b}}>0.

(425)

Let
(A_{b},B_{b})
be the transported symplectic basis. The Riemann bilinear relation for a genus-one Riemann surface gives

\int_{E_{b}}\Omega_{b}\wedge\overline{\Omega_{b}}=\left(\int_{A_{b}}\Omega_{b}\right)\left(\int_{B_{b}}\overline{\Omega_{b}}\right)-\left(\int_{B_{b}}\Omega_{b}\right)\left(\int_{A_{b}}\overline{\Omega_{b}}\right)=\omega_{A}(b)\,\overline{\omega_{B}(b)}-\omega_{B}(b)\,\overline{\omega_{A}(b)}.

(426)

Multiply (
426
) by
i
and use
\alpha-\overline{\alpha}=2i\,\Im(\alpha)
with
\alpha:=\omega_{A}(b)\overline{\omega_{B}(b)}
:

\displaystyle\int_{E_{b}}i\,\Omega_{b}\wedge\overline{\Omega_{b}}

\displaystyle=i\bigl(\omega_{A}\overline{\omega_{B}}-\omega_{B}\overline{\omega_{A}}\bigr)=i\bigl(\omega_{A}\overline{\omega_{B}}-\overline{\omega_{A}\overline{\omega_{B}}}\bigr)=i\cdot 2i\,\Im\bigl(\omega_{A}\overline{\omega_{B}}\bigr)=2\,\Im\bigl(\omega_{A}\overline{\omega_{B}}\bigr).

(427)

By (
425
) and (
427
),
\Im(\omega_{A}\overline{\omega_{B}})>0
. If
\omega_{B}(b)=0
, then
\Im(\omega_{A}\overline{\omega_{B}})=0
, contradiction, hence
\omega_{B}(b)\neq 0
. Finally,

\Im\!\left(\frac{\omega_{A}}{\omega_{B}}\right)=\Im\!\left(\frac{\omega_{A}\overline{\omega_{B}}}{|\omega_{B}|^{2}}\right)=\frac{\Im(\omega_{A}\overline{\omega_{B}})}{|\omega_{B}|^{2}}>0,

(428)

so
\tau(b)\in\mathbb{H}
.
∎

Theorem E.13

(Conditional
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
monodromy and fractional-linear action)
.

Assume
(CA)
. Let
\gamma
be a loop in
B^{\times}
based at
b_{0}
. Let
(A^{\prime}_{b_{0}},B^{\prime}_{b_{0}})
be the transported basis of
H_{1}(E_{b_{0}},\mathbb{Z})
obtained by Gauss-Manin transport along
\gamma
. Then there exists a unique matrix

\rho(\gamma)=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})

(429)

such that

\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}_{b_{0}}\\
B^{\prime}_{b_{0}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A_{b_{0}}\\
B_{b_{0}}\end{pmatrix},

(430)

and the period ratio transforms by

\tau\longmapsto\rho(\gamma)\cdot\tau:=\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}.

(431)

Proof.

Existence and integrality by Lemma
E.9
, transport along
\gamma
yields an automorphism of the free abelian group
H_{1}(E_{b_{0}},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, hence a unique matrix
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in GL(2,\mathbb{Z})
satisfying (
430
). Determinant since transport preserves the intersection pairing,

A^{\prime}_{b_{0}}\cdot B^{\prime}_{b_{0}}=A_{b_{0}}\cdot B_{b_{0}}=1.

(432)

Using bilinearity,
A_{b_{0}}\cdot A_{b_{0}}=B_{b_{0}}\cdot B_{b_{0}}=0
,
A_{b_{0}}\cdot B_{b_{0}}=1
, and
B_{b_{0}}\cdot A_{b_{0}}=-1
, compute

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{b_{0}}\cdot B^{\prime}_{b_{0}}

\displaystyle=(aA_{b_{0}}+bB_{b_{0}})\cdot(cA_{b_{0}}+dB_{b_{0}})

(433)

\displaystyle=ac(A_{b_{0}}\cdot A_{b_{0}})+ad(A_{b_{0}}\cdot B_{b_{0}})+bc(B_{b_{0}}\cdot A_{b_{0}})+bd(B_{b_{0}}\cdot B_{b_{0}})

\displaystyle=0+ad\cdot 1+bc\cdot(-1)+0=ad-bc.

Combining (
432
) and (
433
) gives
ad-bc=1
, hence
\rho(\gamma)\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Fractional-linear action define
\omega_{A}:=\int_{A_{b_{0}}}\Omega_{b_{0}}
,
\omega_{B}:=\int_{B_{b_{0}}}\Omega_{b_{0}}
and similarly
\omega_{A}^{\prime}:=\int_{A^{\prime}_{b_{0}}}\Omega_{b_{0}}
,
\omega_{B}^{\prime}:=\int_{B^{\prime}_{b_{0}}}\Omega_{b_{0}}
. By (
430
) and linearity of integration,

\omega_{A}^{\prime}=a\omega_{A}+b\omega_{B},\qquad\omega_{B}^{\prime}=c\omega_{A}+d\omega_{B}.

(434)

Therefore

\tau^{\prime}=\frac{\omega_{A}^{\prime}}{\omega_{B}^{\prime}}=\frac{a\omega_{A}+b\omega_{B}}{c\omega_{A}+d\omega_{B}}=\frac{a(\omega_{A}/\omega_{B})+b}{c(\omega_{A}/\omega_{B})+d}=\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},

(435)

which is (
431
).
∎

Lemma E.14

(Holomorphic logarithm on a disk)
.

Let
D\subset\mathbb{C}
be a disk and
u:D\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
holomorphic and nowhere vanishing. Then there exists holomorphic
h:D\to\mathbb{C}
such that
e^{h(z)}=u(z)
for all
z\in D
.

Proof.

Since
u
is holomorphic and nowhere vanishing, the holomorphic one-form
(u^{\prime}/u)\,dz
is defined on
D
. Fix
z_{0}\in D
and define

h(z):=\int_{z_{0}}^{z}\frac{u^{\prime}(w)}{u(w)}\,dw+h(z_{0}),

(436)

where the integral is taken along any piecewise
C^{1}
path in
D
. Because
D
is simply connected and
u^{\prime}/u
is holomorphic,
\int_{\partial\Delta}(u^{\prime}/u)\,dw=0
for every triangle
\Delta\subset D
, hence (
436
) is path independent. Differentiating gives
h^{\prime}(z)=u^{\prime}(z)/u(z)
. Then
(e^{h}/u)^{\prime}=(h^{\prime}e^{h}u-e^{h}u^{\prime})/u^{2}=0
, so
e^{h}=Cu
for some constant
C
. Evaluating at
z_{0}
gives
C=1
, hence
e^{h}=u
.
∎

Theorem E.15

(Conditional
T
-monodromy from a simple zero of
q
)
.

Assume
(CA)
and suppose that on a punctured disk
D^{\times}:=D\setminus\{0\}\subset B^{\times}
the elliptic family
\varpi:E\to D^{\times}
admits a Tate uniformization with parameter
q:D^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
satisfying
0<|q(z)|<1
and

q(z)=\exp(2\pi i\,\tau(z))

(437)

on the universal cover of
D^{\times}
. Assume moreover that
q
has a simple zero at
z=0
, i.e. there exists holomorphic
u:D\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
with

q(z)=z\,u(z)\qquad\text{for all }z\in D^{\times}.

(438)

Let
\gamma:[0,2\pi]\to D^{\times}
be the positively oriented loop
\gamma(\theta)=\varepsilon e^{i\theta}
for
0<\varepsilon<\mathrm{radius}(D)
. Then analytic continuation of
\tau
along
\gamma
yields

\tau\longmapsto\tau+1,

(439)

equivalently the monodromy matrix is
T=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
.

Proof.

Work on the universal cover
\widetilde{D^{\times}}
so that a single-valued branch of
\log q
exists. Define

\tau:=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\log q.

(440)

Since
0<|q|<1
, write
\log q=\log|q|+i\arg(q)
with
\log|q|<0
, hence

\Im\tau=\Im\!\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}\log q\right)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|q|>0,

(441)

so
\tau
takes values in
\mathbb{H}
. By (
438
) and Lemma
E.14
, choose a holomorphic logarithm
\log u
on
D
. Along
\gamma
choose the continuous branch
\log(\varepsilon e^{i\theta})=\log\varepsilon+i\theta
for
0\leq\theta\leq 2\pi
. Then along
\gamma
we obtain a continuous determination

\log q(\gamma(\theta))=\log(\varepsilon e^{i\theta})+\log u(\varepsilon e^{i\theta})=\log\varepsilon+i\theta+\log u(\varepsilon e^{i\theta}).

(442)

Evaluate at
\theta=0
and
\theta=2\pi
. Since
\log u
is single-valued on
D
,

\log u(\varepsilon e^{i2\pi})=\log u(\varepsilon e^{i0}),

(443)

and therefore (
442
) gives

\log q(\gamma(2\pi))-\log q(\gamma(0))=\bigl(\log\varepsilon+i2\pi+\log u(\varepsilon)\bigr)-\bigl(\log\varepsilon+i0+\log u(\varepsilon)\bigr)=2\pi i.

(444)

Insert (
444
) into (
440
):

\tau(\gamma(2\pi))-\tau(\gamma(0))=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\bigl(\log q(\gamma(2\pi))-\log q(\gamma(0))\bigr)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\cdot 2\pi i=1,

(445)

which is (
439
). The unique element of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
acting by
\tau\mapsto\tau+1
for all
\tau
is
T=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
because
(a\tau+b)/(c\tau+d)=\tau+1
forces
c=0
,
a=d=1
,
b=1
by comparing coefficients.
∎

Proposition E.16

(Conditional algebraic identity)
.

Let
\tau=C_{0}+ie^{-\phi}
with
C_{0},\phi
real-valued functions and
\Im\tau=e^{-\phi}
. Then the pointwise identity of differential forms holds:

\frac{d\tau\wedge*\,d\overline{\tau}}{2(\Im\tau)^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\,d\phi\wedge*\,d\phi+\frac{1}{2}\,e^{2\phi}\,dC_{0}\wedge*\,dC_{0}.

(446)

Proof.

Compute

d\tau=dC_{0}+i\,d(e^{-\phi})=dC_{0}-ie^{-\phi}d\phi,\qquad d\overline{\tau}=dC_{0}+ie^{-\phi}d\phi.

(447)

Multiply:

\displaystyle d\tau\wedge*\,d\overline{\tau}

\displaystyle=\bigl(dC_{0}-ie^{-\phi}d\phi\bigr)\wedge*\bigl(dC_{0}+ie^{-\phi}d\phi\bigr)

(448)

\displaystyle=dC_{0}\wedge*dC_{0}+ie^{-\phi}dC_{0}\wedge*d\phi-ie^{-\phi}d\phi\wedge*dC_{0}+e^{-2\phi}d\phi\wedge*d\phi.

Using symmetry of the Hodge inner product on one-forms,
dC_{0}\wedge*d\phi=d\phi\wedge*dC_{0}
, the middle two terms cancel:

ie^{-\phi}dC_{0}\wedge*d\phi-ie^{-\phi}d\phi\wedge*dC_{0}=0,

(449)

so

d\tau\wedge*\,d\overline{\tau}=dC_{0}\wedge*dC_{0}+e^{-2\phi}d\phi\wedge*d\phi.

(450)

Divide by
2(\Im\tau)^{2}=2e^{-2\phi}
:

\frac{d\tau\wedge*\,d\overline{\tau}}{2(\Im\tau)^{2}}=\frac{dC_{0}\wedge*dC_{0}}{2e^{-2\phi}}+\frac{e^{-2\phi}d\phi\wedge*d\phi}{2e^{-2\phi}}=\frac{1}{2}e^{2\phi}dC_{0}\wedge*dC_{0}+\frac{1}{2}d\phi\wedge*d\phi,

(451)

which is (
446
).
∎

Definition E.17

(Congruence reductions)
.

Assume
(CA)
and let
\rho:\pi_{1}(B^{\times},b_{0})\to SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
be the monodromy representation from Theorem
E.13
. For each
n\geq 1
define the reduction map
\pi_{n}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
and apply it entrywise to obtain
\pi_{n}:SL(2,\mathbb{Z})\to SL(2,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})
. Define

\rho_{n}:=\pi_{n}\circ\rho:\pi_{1}(B^{\times},b_{0})\to SL(2,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}).

(452)

Proposition E.18

(Compatibility of
\rho_{n}
)
.

Assume
(CA)
. For all
n\geq 1
and all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(B^{\times},b_{0})
one has

\rho_{n+1}(\gamma)\equiv\rho_{n}(\gamma)\pmod{p^{n}}.

(453)

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{red}_{n+1,n}:\mathbb{Z}/p^{n+1}\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
be the canonical quotient map. By definition,
\mathrm{red}_{n+1,n}\circ\pi_{n+1}=\pi_{n}
on
\mathbb{Z}
. Applying this entrywise to a matrix
M\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
yields

\mathrm{red}_{n+1,n}\bigl(\pi_{n+1}(M)\bigr)=\pi_{n}(M).

(454)

With
M=\rho(\gamma)
and using (
452
),

\mathrm{red}_{n+1,n}\bigl(\rho_{n+1}(\gamma)\bigr)=\mathrm{red}_{n+1,n}\bigl(\pi_{n+1}(\rho(\gamma))\bigr)=\pi_{n}(\rho(\gamma))=\rho_{n}(\gamma),

(455)

which is (
453
).
∎

Assuming the varying-
\tau
background data of Definition 7.3 (equivalently the modular map
\underline{\tau}

and the associated elliptic fibration
E(\underline{\tau})\to B^{\times}
with Hodge form
\Omega
), we have:
constructed the period ratio
\tau
on the universal cover with
\Im\tau>0
, proved that Gauss-Manin transport
yields a monodromy representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(B^{\times})\to SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
and the fractional-linear transformation law;
derived the Tate-cusp
T
-monodromy
\tau\mapsto\tau+1
under
q(z)=zu(z)
by explicit logarithmic computation;
established the identity needed when
\partial\tau\neq 0
, and exhibited the compatible congruence reductions

\rho_{n}
.

Appendix F
Varying

\tau
and duality defects

This appendix removes any additional “elliptic-output” axiom in the varying-
\tau

sector by giving an explicit construction of the elliptic fibration

E(\underline{\tau})\to B^{\times}
from the modular map

\underline{\tau}:B^{\times}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,1}=SL(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\mathbb{H}

(equivalently from the pair
(\rho,\widetilde{\tau})
)
and proving the resulting monodromy/defect statements and sourced equations
at the two-derivative bosonic level. We isolate the only analytic input used
in the tower-limit procedure.

Lemma F.1

(Cutoff tower-stability for the circle tower)
.

Let
S^{1}_{(n,p)}
be the
p^{n}
-tower of circle covers used in the definition, and let
\Lambda>0
be a fixed cutoff.
Assume the stage-
n
compactification radius scales as

R_{n}=\frac{R_{0}}{p^{n}},

(456)

as in your operational tower-with-cutoff prescription.
Let
\Delta_{S^{1}_{(n,p)}}
denote the Laplacian on the fiber and let
\mathcal{K}_{\Lambda}^{(n,p)}

be the fiber-mode subspace spanned by eigenmodes with eigenvalue
\leq\Lambda^{2}
.
Then there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(\Lambda,p)
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
,

\mathcal{K}_{\Lambda}^{(n,p)}=\ker(\Delta_{S^{1}_{(n,p)}}),

(457)

and the pullback maps
f_{n}^{*}
identify these kernels canonically. Consequently the truncated field spaces and
cutoff Hilbert spaces stabilize for all
n\geq n_{0}
.

Proof.

On a circle of radius
R_{n}
, the eigenfunctions are
e^{iky/R_{n}}
with eigenvalues
(k/R_{n})^{2}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The first nonzero eigenvalue is
(1/R_{n})^{2}=p^{2n}/R_{0}^{2}
.
Choose
n_{0}
so that
p^{n_{0}}/R_{0}>\Lambda
, i.e.
(1/R_{n_{0}})^{2}>\Lambda^{2}
.
Then for all
n\geq n_{0}
, every
k\neq 0
eigenmode has eigenvalue
(k/R_{n})^{2}\geq(1/R_{n})^{2}>\Lambda^{2}
,
hence is excluded from
\mathcal{K}_{\Lambda}^{(n,p)}
. Therefore
\mathcal{K}_{\Lambda}^{(n,p)}
consists
exactly of the
k=0
mode, i.e.
\ker(\Delta_{S^{1}_{(n,p)}})
.
Since
f_{n}:S^{1}_{(n+1,p)}\to S^{1}_{(n,p)}
is a covering and
f_{n}^{*}(\text{constant})
is constant,
f_{n}^{*}

identifies these kernels canonically for all
n\geq n_{0}
.
This establishes stabilization of the fiber-mode truncation, the stagewise truncations of the full field theory
stabilize accordingly because all retained modes are fiber-constant.
∎

We now construct
E(\tau_{\rm phys})\to B^{\times}
explicitly and show it is holomorphic.

Theorem F.2

(Universal elliptic curve over
\mathbb{H}
and pullback on the universal cover)
.

Let
\widetilde{B^{\times}}
be the universal cover of
B^{\times}
and let

\widetilde{\tau}:\widetilde{B^{\times}}\to\mathbb{H}

be a holomorphic map. Define an action of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
on

\mathbb{C}\times\widetilde{B^{\times}}
by

(m,n)\cdot(z,b):=(z+m+n\,\widetilde{\tau}(b),\,b),\qquad(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

(458)

Then the quotient

\widetilde{E}:=(\mathbb{C}\times\widetilde{B^{\times}})/\mathbb{Z}^{2}

(459)

is a complex manifold and the projection
\widetilde{\varpi}:\widetilde{E}\to\widetilde{B^{\times}}
is a proper holomorphic submersion
whose fiber over
b
is canonically isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\widetilde{\tau}(b)\mathbb{Z})
.
Moreover,
\widetilde{E}
carries a holomorphic line subbundle (the Hodge line) generated locally by the relative form
dz
.

Proof.

Fix
b\in\widetilde{B^{\times}}
. Since
\Im\widetilde{\tau}(b)>0
, the subgroup

\Lambda_{b}:=\mathbb{Z}+\widetilde{\tau}(b)\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{C}
is a rank-2 lattice.
Thus
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
acts freely and properly discontinuously on
\mathbb{C}\times\{b\}
by translations,
and the quotient is the elliptic curve
\mathbb{C}/\Lambda_{b}
.
Because the action is free, properly discontinuous, and holomorphic in
(z,b)
, the global quotient

\widetilde{E}
is a complex manifold and the induced projection to
\widetilde{B^{\times}}
is holomorphic.
Properness and submersion follow fiberwise locally in
b
,
\widetilde{\tau}
varies holomorphically, hence

\Lambda_{b}
varies continuously, and the quotient by a lattice is a proper holomorphic submersion.
Finally,
dz
is invariant under translations
z\mapsto z+m+n\widetilde{\tau}(b)
, hence defines a nowhere-vanishing
relative holomorphic form on each fiber, well-defined up to multiplication by a nowhere-vanishing holomorphic function of
b
,
i.e. it determines the Hodge line bundle.
∎

To descend from
\widetilde{B^{\times}}
to
B^{\times}
we encode monodromy.

Theorem F.3

(Descent and induced
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
monodromy)
.

Let
\Gamma:=\pi_{1}(B^{\times},b_{0})
act on
\widetilde{B^{\times}}
by deck transformations.
Assume there exists a representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(B^{\times},b_{0})\to SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
such that for every deck
transformation
\gamma
and every
b\in\widetilde{B^{\times}}
one has the equivariance condition

\tilde{\tau}(\gamma\cdot b)=\rho(\gamma)\cdot\tilde{\tau}(b)=\frac{a\tilde{\tau}(b)+b}{c\tilde{\tau}(b)+d},\qquad\rho(\gamma)=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z}).

(460)

Define an action of
\Gamma
on
\mathbb{C}\times\widetilde{B^{\times}}
by

\gamma\cdot(z,b):=\left(\frac{z}{c\widetilde{\tau}(b)+d},\,\gamma\cdot b\right).

(461)

Then the combined action of the semidirect product
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rtimes_{\rho}\Gamma
on
\mathbb{C}\times\widetilde{B^{\times}}

is properly discontinuous and holomorphic, and the quotient

E(\tau_{\rm phys}):=(\mathbb{C}\times\widetilde{B^{\times}})/(\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rtimes_{\rho}\Gamma)

(462)

is a complex orbifold (a manifold if
\rho
avoids elliptic points) equipped with a holomorphic map

\varpi:E(\tau_{\rm phys})\to B^{\times}
whose fiber over
b
is canonically
\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\tau_{\rm phys}(b)\mathbb{Z})
.
The Gauss-Manin monodromy of
R^{1}\varpi_{*}\mathbb{Z}
equals
\rho
.

Proof.

We first show compatibility for
(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

\gamma\circ(m,n)\circ\gamma^{-1}

(463)

acts on
(z,b)
by translation by a new pair
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
determined by
\rho(\gamma)
.
Indeed, compute using
\gamma^{-1}\cdot(z,b)=((c\widetilde{\tau}(\gamma^{-1}b)+d)z,\gamma^{-1}b)
and the definition of the

\mathbb{Z}^{2}
action:

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot\big((m,n)\cdot(\gamma^{-1}\cdot(z,b))\big)

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot\big((c\widetilde{\tau}(\gamma^{-1}b)+d)z+m+n\widetilde{\tau}(\gamma^{-1}b),\,\gamma^{-1}b\big)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{(c\widetilde{\tau}(\gamma^{-1}b)+d)z+m+n\widetilde{\tau}(\gamma^{-1}b)}{c\widetilde{\tau}(\gamma^{-1}b)+d},\,b\right)

\displaystyle=\left(z+\frac{m+n\widetilde{\tau}(\gamma^{-1}b)}{c\widetilde{\tau}(\gamma^{-1}b)+d},\,b\right).

(464)

Using
\widetilde{\tau}(\gamma^{-1}b)=\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\widetilde{\tau}(b)
, one checks the standard identity

\frac{m+n\,(\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\tau)}{c(\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\tau)+d}=m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}\tau

(465)

with
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})=(m,n)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Thus
\Gamma
normalizes the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
translations, and the combined action is a semidirect product action.
Proper discontinuity follows because
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
acts properly discontinuously on each fiber and
\Gamma
acts properly discontinuously
on the base, holomorphicity is immediate from the formulas.
The quotient therefore defines a holomorphic family over
B^{\times}
with the claimed fibers.
Finally,
R^{1}\varpi_{*}\mathbb{Z}
is the local system of the first homology of fibers, the above descent uses
\rho

as the gluing of homology bases, hence the Gauss-Manin monodromy is exactly
\rho
.
∎

Corollary F.4

(Derivation of the former “Comparison Axiom”)
.

Given a varying-
\tau
background in the sense of Definition (
7.6
)
(equivalently, a modular map

\underline{\tau}:B^{\times}\to SL(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\mathbb{H}

or an equivariant pair
(\rho,\widetilde{\tau})
on the universal cover),
the elliptic fibration
\varpi:E(\underline{\tau})\to B^{\times}

exists canonically by Theorems (
F.2
)-Theorem (
F.3
). Hence the varying-
\tau
sector
does not require any additional elliptic-output axiom the elliptic
fibration is determined by
\underline{\tau}
.

We now prove the local 7-brane monodromy from first principles.

Proposition F.5

(
T^{n}
monodromy from logarithmic singularities)
.

Let
D
be a small disk with coordinate
z
and
D^{\times}=D\setminus\{0\}
.
Assume

\tau(z)=\frac{n}{2\pi i}\log z+h(z),

(466)

where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
h
is holomorphic on
D
.
Then analytic continuation around the positively oriented loop
z=\varepsilon e^{i\theta}
induces

\tau\ \longmapsto\ \tau+n,

(467)

hence the monodromy matrix is
T^{n}
with

T:=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(468)

Proof.

Choose a branch of
\log z
on the universal cover of
D^{\times}
. Under
\theta:0\to 2\pi
,

\log z\mapsto\log z+2\pi i
. Therefore

\tau\mapsto\frac{n}{2\pi i}(\log z+2\pi i)+h(z)=\tau+n.

(469)

The unique
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
matrix acting by
\tau\mapsto\tau+n
is
T^{n}
.
∎

Your paper already records the bosonic IIB action with the
\tau
kinetic term.
We now vary it explicitly and show how logarithmic singularities correspond to codimension-two sources.

Proposition F.6

(Equation of motion for
\tau
and delta sources)
.

Consider the bosonic Type IIB Lagrangian density (with
G^{(3)}=0
and
F^{(5)}=0
for simplicity)

\mathcal{L}=\sqrt{-g}\left(R-\frac{\partial_{\mu}\tau\,\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}}{2(\Im\tau)^{2}}\right).

(470)

Treating
\tau
and
\overline{\tau}
as independent fields, the Euler-Lagrange equation for
\tau
is

\nabla_{\mu}\left(\frac{\partial^{\mu}\tau}{(\Im\tau)^{2}}\right)=0\qquad\text{on smooth points.}

(471)

If
\tau
has a logarithmic singularity as in Proposition
F.5
along a divisor component,
the equation holds on
B^{\times}
but acquires a delta-function source supported on
\Delta
with coefficient
n
.

Proof.

Write
y:=\Im\tau=(\tau-\overline{\tau})/(2i)
, so
\delta y=\delta\tau/(2i)
when varying
\tau
with
\overline{\tau}
fixed.
Vary the kinetic term:

\displaystyle\delta\left(\frac{\partial_{\mu}\tau\,\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}}{2y^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2y^{2}}\,\partial_{\mu}(\delta\tau)\,\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}+\frac{\partial_{\mu}\tau\,\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}}{2}\,\delta(y^{-2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2y^{2}}\,\partial_{\mu}(\delta\tau)\,\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}-\frac{\partial_{\mu}\tau\,\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}}{y^{3}}\,\delta y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2y^{2}}\,\partial_{\mu}(\delta\tau)\,\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}-\frac{\partial_{\mu}\tau\,\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}}{2i\,y^{3}}\,\delta\tau.

(472)

Integrating by parts and discarding boundary terms on
B^{\times}
yields

\delta S=-\int d^{10}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,\delta\tau\left[\nabla_{\mu}\left(\frac{\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}}{2y^{2}}\right)+\frac{\partial_{\mu}\tau\,\partial^{\mu}\overline{\tau}}{2i\,y^{3}}\right].

(473)

Taking complex conjugate gives the
\overline{\tau}
equation.
Equivalently, rewriting the bracket as
\nabla_{\mu}(\partial^{\mu}\tau/y^{2})
gives the stated form for
\tau

(after standard algebra using
\nabla_{\mu}y=(\nabla_{\mu}\tau-\nabla_{\mu}\overline{\tau})/(2i)
).
For a logarithmic singularity
\tau=\frac{n}{2\pi i}\log z+\text{holomorphic}
in a transverse complex coordinate
z
,
the distributional identity
\partial\overline{\partial}\log|z|^{2}=2\pi i\,\delta^{(2)}(z)\,dz\wedge d\overline{z}

implies the divergence equation acquires a delta source with coefficient
n
.
∎

To complete the “full F-theory” definition, one must extend
E(\tau_{\rm phys})
across
\Delta
as a relatively minimal
Weierstrass model and identify fiber types/monodromy classes. We package this as a standard theorem application.

Theorem F.7

(Existence of relatively minimal Weierstrass model and Kodaira fiber types)
.

Assume
B
is smooth and that the modular map

\underline{\tau}:B^{\times}\to SL(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\mathbb{H}

has at worst cusp-type/logarithmic growth along
\Delta
, so that

j\circ\underline{\tau}
extends meromorphically to
B
.
Then
E(\tau_{\rm phys})\to B^{\times}
extends (after possibly birational modification over
\Delta
) to a relatively minimal
Weierstrass model
y^{2}=x^{3}+fx+g
over
B
with discriminant divisor
\Delta=\{4f^{3}+27g^{2}=0\}
,
and the local monodromy conjugacy class in
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
around each irreducible component of
\Delta

matches the Kodaira fiber type at that component.

Proof.

This is the standard extension/classification theorem for elliptic fibrations with meromorphic
j
-invariant:
one constructs a Weierstrass model from
j
(equivalently from
(f,g)
) and uses minimality to obtain the Kodaira list.
The monodromy statement follows because the Gauss-Manin local system
R^{1}\varpi_{*}\mathbb{Z}
is the homology local system of the fiber,
and Kodaira’s classification computes its local monodromy (up to conjugacy) for each singular fiber type.
∎

By Theorems
F.2
-
F.3
, the elliptic fibration over
B^{\times}
is
constructed

from
\tau_{\rm phys}
and its monodromy, so the varying-
\tau
sector does not require an additional elliptic-output axiom.
Proposition
F.5
gives explicit local defect monodromy, and Proposition
F.6
ties logarithmic singularities to
codimension-two sources in the two-derivative IIB equations. Theorem
F.7
completes the global extension and fiber-type
classification as a standard theorem application once
j(\tau_{\rm phys})
extends meromorphically.
